# Chapter 1 — The Catch

## The Weight of It

Sable Ro knew the Bathysphere *Meridian* by the weight of it.

Not the weight recorded in the dockmaster's logs — forty-three tonnes, pressure-rated to six hundred atmospheres — but the weight she felt through the soles of her boots as she crossed the loading gantry: the particular give of the deck plate over frame seven, the slight counter-resonance of the ballast tanks cycling on her left side, a frequency her body had catalogued long before her mind had thought to name it.

She had worked this ship for three seasons. She knew it the way she knew everything: through what it did to her skin.

The gantry retracted. The docking seal engaged — she felt it as a low, pressurized thud transmitted up through the hull into the deck, a door closing at the bottom of an ocean — and then the *Meridian* was sealed and Coda's dock noise was gone. Not that she had heard it. But the irregular vibrations of a working dock floor — the footfall of technicians, the tremor of freight loaders, the deep pulse of the colony's atmospheric processors communicated through the structure of the station itself — those had been underneath her for forty minutes while she ran pre-dive checks, and now they were not, and the silence of her body's surface was different.

Sable moved through the forward compartment toward the cradle without looking at her checklist. The checklist was for Thresh.

The sensory cradle occupied the aft third of the dive cabin — a contoured frame of impact-dampening gel over structural carbon, calibrated to her body's contact map over three seasons of progressive adjustment. It was not comfortable. Nothing about the lower atmosphere of Vespers was designed for comfort. But the cradle distributed pressure load evenly enough across her back and shoulders and the long column of her spine that the dermal transducers grafted between her vertebrae — seven of them, each the diameter of a thumbnail, housing a cluster of acoustic-to-haptic translation nodes — could operate without the white noise of structural stress contaminating their input feeds.

She backed into it. Felt the gel conform. Reached up and engaged the left shoulder restraint, then the right, then the lap harness, threading the buckle by touch without looking. Her fingertips — all ten, where additional transducer nodes were embedded beneath the skin and connected to the spinal array via subcutaneous filament — registered the texture of the harness webbing: synthetic polymer over a braided steel core, slightly tacky from the cabin's humidity.

The dive cabin lights dimmed to operational amber. Not for her benefit.

Thresh came through the forward hatch, already suited below the waist, still pulling on his upper thermals. He was tall enough that he had to angle his shoulders through the frame, a habit so ingrained he did not think about it. He saw her strapped in and stopped.

His left hand came up. Index and middle fingers extended, wrist turned outward: *ready?*

She tapped two fingers against her left collarbone. *Ready.*

This was not the sign language that came on the language modules in the colony's education system. That language was standardized, designed for surface environments — broad gestures with generous visual margins. What Sable and Thresh used was specific to this cabin, to these light levels, to the particular problem of communicating in gloves with one hand often occupied. Thresh had learned it from her predecessor on the *Meridian*, a harvester named Doss who had lost two fingers on his left hand in a net-winding accident and had adapted accordingly. Sable had refined it. Thresh had adopted every refinement without complaint.

He moved to the operations console. The overhead strip lighting shifted — a brief pulse of blue-white, the pre-dive confirmation sequence — and she felt the *Meridian's* drive system engage through the cradle's gel pads: a sound in her bones before it was a motion in the ship. The drive frequency was a familiar texture, a coarse granularity that started at her lower back and distributed upward along the spinal transducers in a cascade she had mapped precisely enough to identify specific harmonics. The port thruster ran slightly rough in cold atmosphere — it always had, since a storm particulate ingestion event in her second season. Not dangerous. Documented. Known.

The gantry release fired. The *Meridian* dropped.

Not sharply — the magnetic assist rails of Coda's lower dock held the ship's descent for the first two hundred meters, feeding velocity incrementally until the atmospheric pressure differential could take over. She felt the rail contact as a vibrating lateral press against both sides of the hull simultaneously, evenly distributed, like being held. Then the rails ended and the ship was in open air and the holding-feeling resolved into something else: a vast, low-frequency vibration that was not the ship at all but the atmosphere itself — the weather systems of Vespers pressing against the *Meridian's* hull from every direction, each pressure wave arriving at a different vector, their sum a complex tactile environment she had spent three seasons learning to read.

The descent began in the upper stratospheric band. Up here the gas was relatively thin — thin being a relative term when the gravity was 2.4 standard — and the pressure registered at the hull as a soft, high-frequency shimmer across her back, fast and light, like rain on a roof. Through the viewport the sky was bruised. Purple at high altitudes gave way to a deeper violet as the ship fell, then to a banded amber-gold where the first major cloud layer began. The colors were saturated in the way of things lit from within — not reflected light but a chemical luminescence that rose from the lower bands where molecular hydrogen underwent exotic pressure states and released photons as a byproduct of its own compression.

She watched it. She always watched the descent. The harvesting was paid work, was quota and contracts and the slow accumulation of transfer credits, but the descent was something else. She had never found a word for it in any language she knew.

The *Meridian* passed through the first cloud layer. The viewport went opaque with particulate, then cleared as the external wash-jets removed the condensate, and the light that came through was gold now — deep and warm, the color of something aged. The pressure against the hull had shifted: the high-frequency shimmer was gone, replaced by a heavier, lower-register vibration she felt in her ribs more than her back. The atmosphere thickening. Still well within operational parameters. They had not yet reached crush depth.

Thresh looked over his shoulder. Held up four fingers — four minutes to trawl deployment altitude — then pointed downward. She tapped her collarbone twice. *Acknowledged.*

The *Meridian* continued to fall.

## Crush Depth

Crush depth was not a precise number.

The *Meridian's* technical specifications listed a rated operational minimum of four hundred and twelve standard atmospheres, below which structural integrity degradation became nonlinear. The dockmaster's briefing listed trawl deployment at three hundred to three-fifty. Sable's personal operational floor, developed over three seasons of empirical calibration, was three hundred and twenty-eight atmospheres — the point at which the interplay of the hull's stress harmonics and the atmospheric pressure wave pattern generated a specific tactile signature at her spinal transducers: a sustained, low warmth, like a hand pressed flat against the back of her neck. She had learned to trust it as a limit more precisely than any gauge.

The depth gauge ticked past three hundred and fifteen. The warmth at her neck was beginning.

Thresh was already at the net controls. The fractal-mesh deployment system occupied the *Meridian's* entire ventral surface — a series of nested panels that opened in sequence, allowing the nets to unfurl beneath the ship like the petals of a flower in time-lapse. The mesh itself was not physical in the conventional sense. It was a field-state array — a lattice of coherence resonators that, when activated, created a zone of acoustic impedance differential around the ship, a region where the normal propagation of sound waves through the high-pressure gas was interrupted. Acoustic fossils — frozen wavefronts, sound compressed into semi-crystalline states by the temperature differentials of Vespers' storm layers over centuries or millennia — drifted in these deep bands like sediment, and the mesh was designed to catch them: to destabilize their crystalline matrix enough that they could be drawn into the capture pods and re-frozen at controlled temperature for transport.

It was, Sable had always thought, less like fishing and more like archaeology. You disturbed the sediment and hoped what came up was worth the fuel cost.

Thresh ran the deployment sequence. She felt the ventral panels open through the hull — a series of low-frequency thuds, spaced three seconds apart, transmitted up through the frame into the gel pad beneath her — and then the coherence resonators came online and the *Meridian* changed. The way the atmosphere pressed against the ship shifted subtly: the resonator field altered the local acoustic environment, and the feedback loop between the field and her spinal transducers meant that the background tactile noise of the deep atmosphere became slightly more complex, slightly more textured, a crowded signal she had learned to parse.

She set her hands flat on the armrest pads. Closed her eyes.

What came through in the first minutes of a trawl was mostly noise. The deep atmosphere of Vespers was extraordinarily acoustically active — pressure waves from storm systems thousands of kilometers in diameter, cavitation events from the boundary layers between gas bands of different density, molecular vibration from chemical reactions occurring at scales she could not quite conceptualize. Her transducers filtered for the signature of crystallized acoustic fossils: the slightly wrong density, the coherence that distinguished a frozen wavefront from ambient chaos, the specific frequency signature of compressed acoustic history.

The fossils arrived one by one.

The first, at 11:04 — a small impact at the center of her right palm, brief and clean, a texture like brushed aluminum, cool. Small-mass event, probably a local impact signature, somewhere in the hundred-to-two-hundred-year range given the crystal density implied by the signal texture. Standard. Common. Worth approximately forty Archive credits per gram at current commodity rates.

The second, four minutes later. Both palms simultaneously, a wash of warmth that was not temperature but pressure-frequency — a broad wavefront event, something that had propagated across a wide area. The warmth built and faded in a pattern she recognized: a stellar nursery signature. The formation event of a star system somewhere in the galaxy's arm, its acoustic signature trapped in Vespers' crystalline storms and held there while the star in question had probably lived its entire billion-year life and died and been forgotten. She felt it as warmth. She filed it. Forty-three Archive credits base, with premium for stellar provenance documentation.

The third fossil was nothing. Debris artifact from a system impact event. She let it pass through.

The fourth — at 11:22, eleven minutes into the trawl — was something she had felt only once before, in her first season, on a different ship with a different rig. It came through both palms and all ten fingertips simultaneously: an initial sharp pinprick of cold followed by a sustained, structured pressure pattern that built in a spiral across her finger pads. She had felt it before and had known then what it was, but she opened her eyes anyway, because she liked to see the documentation confirm what her skin already knew.

The display read: *CANDIDATE — COSMIC EVENT CLASS — SUPERNOVA SIGNATURE — ESTIMATED AGE: 4.2B YEARS — CRYSTAL DENSITY: 0.87.*

Four-point-two billion years old. The last acoustic signature of a dying star, compressed by Vespers' weather into something she could hold in her hands, if her hands had not been bolted to an armrest beneath a gas giant's pressure.

She allowed herself six seconds of something that was not quite feeling and not quite thought. Then she flagged it for the premium capture pod and returned to the trawl.

Thresh looked over. His eyebrows rose.

She held up two fingers — *supernova, class two* — and he exhaled visibly and turned back to the console to adjust the capture routing.

The warmth at her neck was deeper now. Three hundred and twenty-six atmospheres. Approaching her personal floor.

She gave the trawl four more minutes. The depth gauge crept. The atmosphere pressed.

At 11:31, the mesh caught something.

She felt it before the console flagged it — felt it as an absence first, which was wrong. One moment the fractal-mesh feedback was its usual complex presence across all transducer nodes, and then a fraction of a second later something hit the field and the feedback — the tactile environment she had been reading continuously for thirty-seven minutes — simply stopped. Not faded. Stopped. Every node across her spine and her fingertips went simultaneously and completely silent.

In three seasons, the transducers had never gone silent.

For 0.9 seconds she experienced something she had no technical category for. Her skin was producing no information. The world had gone away.

Then the signal returned.

## Not a Sound

It was not a sound.

She understood sound through its translations — the warmth of a stellar birth, the ice-water pinprick of an impact event, the structured spiral pressure of a supernova's shockwave compressed into crystal. She had a complete vocabulary for sound built entirely from what it did to her skin, and she had spent three seasons learning to trust that vocabulary absolutely.

What came through the transducers now had no entry in that vocabulary.

The first impression was geometric. A shape — not a metaphor, not a sensation that resembled a shape, but something that occupied spatial dimensions within her nervous system and pressed against them from the inside. It arrived at her spine and her fingertips simultaneously, which was normal, but the signal was not distributed the way signals distributed. Normally an acoustic fossil fed through the nodes in sequence, the spinal array providing density and frequency data while the fingertip nodes provided texture and temporal structure. This was not sequential. This was simultaneous and total — every node firing at once — and the sum of it was a shape she did not have a name for. Something with too many angles. Something that folded inward at its corners. Something that pressed and pressed and did not stop pressing.

She registered, secondarily, that it hurt.

Pain was not something the transducers were supposed to produce. They were signal-translation devices, not sensory amplifiers; the input was filtered, range-limited, incapable by design of exceeding the nervous system's pain threshold. She had never felt pain through the transducers. She felt it now: a burning that was not the burning of heat but the burning of geometry — something shaped wrong occupying her nerve pathways, a fire built from angles.

Her hands locked on the armrests. She was aware of this happening without being able to prevent it.

The shape grew. Not louder — it had no volume in any conventional sense, had no amplitude she could identify — but *larger*, expanding within the transducer network the way a sound expands in a room, filling available space, reflecting off the interior surfaces of her nervous system and coming back changed, more complex, more folded. The angles multiplied. There were harmonics within the shape, sub-structures she could not track because the primary shape was already more than her processing could organize, and beneath the harmonics was something else, something she kept returning to even as the pain tried to pull her attention away—

She became aware that the visual alarms were active.

She opened her eyes. The cabin was red. Not just the alarm strips — the operational amber had been replaced by emergency red across every surface, and the console display was a cascade of warning flags she could not read because her visual processing was occupied with the effort of not being consumed by what was happening in her spine.

Thresh was at the console. Both hands moving fast. His mouth was open — she could see the shape of it, the expression she associated with him using his voice, which she had never heard and imagined as something low and even — and his eyes moved from the display to her and back to the display.

The shape in her spine had a texture now. Beneath the geometry, beneath the folded angles and the burning, there was something that she tried to classify and could not. Not a data signature. Not the acoustic residue of a physical event. Something that had been *directed.* Something that had been, at some moment she could not locate in any time she knew, produced deliberately. Aimed. *Meant.*

She had felt the deaths of stars. She had felt the formation of solar systems. She had felt events from four billion years in the past as texture in her fingertips, and none of them had ever felt like they were *for* something. Physics had no direction. Acoustics had no intention.

This had intention.

The shape compressed — a sudden violent contraction, every node firing inward simultaneously — and then expanded again, and the expansion carried something that she would later, in her notes, describe only as: *a voice.* Not a voice she heard. A voice her nervous system recognized as a voice the way a hand recognizes a hand when it touches one in the dark. A voice that was panicked. A voice that was, in whatever language physics allowed for the expression of panic, *mid-sentence.*

The burning reached a peak she had no scale for.

Then the capture pod engaged — she felt the resonator field shift as the mesh directed the fossil toward the containment array, felt the crystalline matrix begin to re-freeze around the captured wavefront — and the signal dropped to a fraction of its former intensity.

Not gone. Not safe. But no longer total.

She realized her hands had torn the armrest pad covering on the left side. She had not felt herself do it. The synthetic material was in pieces around her fingers. She looked at it for a moment, then at her hands, then at Thresh.

Thresh's face was doing something she did not have a sign for.

She tried to compose her expression into something operational. She was not certain she succeeded.

## The Ascent

The *Meridian* ascended at reduced thrust. Standard post-capture protocol required a controlled climb to minimize crystal fracture risk in the capture pods — slower than Sable wanted.

She had unstrapped from the cradle. She was standing in the middle of the dive cabin, not because she needed to be standing, but because the gel pad had the torn armrest covering on one side and she did not want to sit in the evidence of what her body had done without her permission. She held her hands at her sides and looked at the console displays in the way of someone who is reading them without constructing meaning — taking in numbers, not building facts — which was as close as she came to not thinking.

Her transducers were running a post-exposure diagnostic. She could feel it: a systematic, low-level pulse through each node in sequence, like a doctor pressing on joints to assess damage. Most nodes were reporting within normal range. Two fingertip nodes on her right hand were flagging a sensitivity reduction — not permanent damage, probably, but a recalibration would be required before the next dive. The spinal nodes were intact but running warm. They should not be running warm. They were passive translation hardware. They did not generate heat.

She made a note of it and filed it in the part of her mind designated for problems to solve when the immediate situation permitted.

Thresh was at the console, not running diagnostics or filing capture documentation. He was standing with one hand on the edge of it and the other turning a small calibration tool between his fingers — not using it, just turning it, the way he always did when his hands needed to be doing something and there was nothing useful for them to do. His back was to her — which was unusual. Thresh did not turn his back to her in the cabin. It was a habit they had developed without discussing it. The fundamental operating principle of working with someone who was deaf was that you did not put your back to them, because your back could not sign.

She crossed to the console and put her hand on his shoulder.

He turned. His face had resolved into something she recognized — not the expression she had filed under *examine later*, but a professional composure that was working harder than professional composure usually had to work.

His right hand: *capture pod nominal. Fossil secured.*

She tapped her collarbone. *Good.*

His left hand joined his right: *what did you—* He paused. His hands reorganized. *How did it feel?*

She thought about this for several seconds.

She made the sign for *wrong* — dominant hand flat, palm down, a single rotation at the wrist — and then added the modifier she used for things that were wrong in ways that exceeded category. It was a small sign, almost no sign at all: a particular stillness of both hands after the first one that meant *wrong in a way I don't have language for yet.*

He held her gaze for a moment and then looked back at the console, and she could see the set of his shoulders change in a way she had learned to read as: *acknowledged; not pursuing now.*

The depth gauge read one hundred and twelve atmospheres. The ascent continued.

Sable turned to look at the capture pod housing — a sealed cylinder mounted to the aft bulkhead, approximately half a meter in diameter, temperature-regulated, vibration-isolated, displaying a green status light that meant the fossil was secured and the crystal matrix was stable. She looked at it the way she had been trained not to look at things she was paid to deliver: with something that was trying to be more than professional assessment.

The fossil was in there. Whatever it was. Whatever its shape had meant, pressing through her nervous system in a language she did not have.

The status light was green.

She was still looking at it when she noticed the condensation.

The *Meridian's* inner hull ran a light moisture problem in descent — a temperature differential between the heated cabin and the cold hull plating created a thin condensate film along the port-side wall. She had noted it in her maintenance log six months ago; the dock engineer had sealed the thermal break adequately but not perfectly, and a hairline continued to admit enough thermal transfer to produce two or three millimeters of condensate on the affected section of wall during every deep dive.

She knew this section of wall. She had looked at it several hundred times.

The condensate droplet she was looking at now was falling upward.

Not fast. Not dramatically. A single drop, approximately two millimeters in diameter, moving slowly up the wall from a point three centimeters above the floor, traveling toward the ceiling at roughly four centimeters per second. It was not flowing — not a trick of the wall's surface, not capillary action or static charge, both of which she had encountered in various forms aboard various ships. It was falling upward with the casual, absolute indifference of an object obeying gravity.

She stood and looked at it.

It reached the ceiling. It did not splash. It adhered for a moment — she saw the slight deformation of its surface as it contacted the overhead panel — and then it simply stopped being visible, as if it had been absorbed, or as if she had stopped seeing it, though she had not moved her eyes.

She looked at the wall below where it had been.

On the seam between hull plates, where the thermal break hairline ran — a section of wall she could have described from memory with enough accuracy to draft a technical illustration — there was rust. Not the familiar slow oxidation she had been watching develop for six months. This was different: a bloom of rust spreading from the seam across approximately eight centimeters of hull plating, orange-brown and vivid, the kind of oxidation that took years in a salt environment. She watched it spread for perhaps two seconds.

Then it was gone. The plating was clean. The hairline seam showed its familiar slight discoloration from six months of moisture and nothing else.

Sable held her position. Hands at her sides. Her transducers running their post-exposure diagnostic pulse — steady, sequential, each node confirming its own integrity in the only language she had ever trusted: the language of sensation, of what the world did to her skin.

She looked at the wall. She looked at the space where the rust had been and was not.

She catalogued it the way she catalogued everything. The observation, the duration, the absence of any explanation within her current operational framework, the fact that Thresh had been facing the console and had not seen it. She held these data points in sequence and did not impose meaning on them — not yet — because meaning required a framework and she did not yet have one.

She had felt the shape of something that was not a sound. She had felt intention in physics. She had watched a drop of water obey a different gravity and watched a year's worth of entropy bloom and vanish in two seconds on a wall she knew by heart.

She filed it. She returned her attention to the ascent.

The depth gauge read eighty-eight atmospheres. The light through the viewport was shifting from the deep gold of the lower bands back toward violet — the bruised color of higher altitude, of almost-safe.

She looked at the capture pod housing.

The status light was still green.

Somewhere in the sealed cylinder, in a crystal matrix held at controlled temperature against the pressure of the ship's return, something that was not a sound waited in the particular silence of things that have been caught.

# Chapter 2 — Coda

## Return to Coda

Coda announced itself before it was visible.

At sixty atmospheres the colony's docking beacons began their low-frequency pulse — a navigational signal transmitted through the atmospheric medium rather than through space, felt rather than seen, a rhythmic pressure against the hull that Sable had learned in her first week aboard the *Meridian* to distinguish from weather and from the ship's own systems. It came in threes: two short pulses and one long, repeating every forty seconds. She felt it now as a faint but recognizable pattern across her lower back as she stood at the viewport, watching the bruised violet of the upper atmosphere give way to the amber glow of Coda's outer illumination ring.

The colony occupied a stable pressure band at approximately thirty-one standard atmospheres — high enough above the crushing depths to allow structural integrity without constant reinforcement cycling, low enough to use the gas giant's atmosphere as both thermal insulation and radiation shielding. From the outside, approaching from below, it looked like something that had no right to exist: a horizontal structure nearly two kilometers across, its underside a maze of docking cradles and equipment gantries and the enormous pressure-differential membranes that kept the inhabited sections sealed against the atmosphere. The outer ring was industrial — storage, processing, the acoustic fossil refinement labs where raw crystalline wavefronts were prepared for Archive cataloguing and commercial sale. The inner ring was residential, administrative, the Archive's primary facility.

Sable had docked at Coda forty-seven times. She had counted, once, in her second season, when she had needed something precise to think about during a difficult ascent.

She knew the approach by its vibration signature. The docking cradle locks fired in sequence — three impacts, each transmitted through the hull as a distinct percussive thud, each slightly different in timbre because each cradle was slightly different in age and wear. The magnetic grapples that secured the ship's dorsal hull to the cradle framework engaged with a sustained pressure against the top of the ship, felt at her feet as a low, even compression, the vibration of the electromagnets cycling at their holding frequency.

Then the pressurization bridge connected, and the vibration changed entirely.

A city's worth of mechanical systems communicated themselves through the structure of Coda's dock. Not cleanly — it was nothing like the *Meridian's* known harmonics, nothing she had mapped or named — but in the aggregate it was familiar. Forty-seven dockings had given her a composite sense of what Coda felt like, the way a long-time resident of a house develops an unconscious awareness of its particular creaks. She knew when the atmospheric processors in sector four were running a maintenance cycle. She knew the particular shudder of the freight elevator along the colony's spine. She knew the difference between a normal docking floor and a docking floor with a crowd on it.

The dock felt normal. She had half-expected it not to.

Thresh came through the forward hatch with the post-dive documentation on a tablet, already dressed out of his dive suit, moving with the slightly stiff posture of someone who had been in an uncomfortable position for too long. He handed her the tablet without preamble. She read it.

The standard post-dive fields were complete: dive depth, trawl duration, atmospheric conditions, equipment status, capture pod inventory. Two entries under *Captures:* one supernova fossil, class two, crystal density 0.87, four-point-two billion years estimated age, premium classification, Archive credit valuation pending specialist review. One anomalous capture, classification pending, crystal density — and here the field was blank, replaced with a flag she had not seen before in three seasons of post-dive documentation.

The flag read: *CLASSIFICATION SYSTEM UNABLE TO PROCESS. MANUAL REVIEW REQUIRED.*

She looked at the flag for a moment. Then she looked at Thresh.

His hands: *Archive sent a receipt acknowledgment for the anomaly thirty minutes ago. Standard intake protocol. They want us in processing as soon as the ship is secured.*

She handed the tablet back. *Did they say why the classification failed?*

*No.*

She looked at the capture pod housing. The status light was still green. Had been green since 11:31. Whatever was in there — whatever shape had pressed through her nervous system and burned geometric fire along her nerve pathways — was now a bureaucratic problem for the Pan-Systemic Archive's intake department.

She picked up her jacket from the equipment rack. She put it on. She followed Thresh to the docking bridge.

## The Bridge

The bridge connecting the *Meridian* to Coda's dock was enclosed and pressurized — a standard telescoping umbilical, approximately fifteen meters long, lit by overhead fluorescents that buzzed at a frequency she felt as a faint, fast vibration in the bridge's deck plating. She had walked this bridge forty-seven times. The fluorescent buzz was always there. She had not consciously noticed it until now, because she was noticing everything now, cataloguing every sensation against the possibility that something was wrong with the catalogue itself.

The transducers were still running warm.

Not hot — not painful, not the burning geometry of the anomaly's signal. Just warm. A degree or two above their normal operating temperature, which should have been ambient, which should have been whatever temperature the surrounding tissue was. Passive hardware did not generate heat. She had noted it aboard the ship. She was noting it again now, with more precision, because the *Meridian* was behind her and she was walking through a structure she had walked through forty-seven times and she wanted to be certain that what she was feeling was the transducers and not the bridge.

It was the transducers. The bridge felt exactly as it always felt. The fluorescent buzz at her feet, the slight flex of the umbilical structure against the docking ring in the medium-pressure wind event currently running at approximately 140 kilometers per hour outside the colony's weather shields, the texture of the non-slip deck surface through her boot soles.

All known. All catalogued. All correct.

She walked through the docking bay's pressure door and into the colony proper, and the smell of it met her the way it always did — recycled atmosphere with a slight metallic edge, a trace of the chemical compounds used in the acoustic refinement labs on the outer ring, something organic underneath that she had always assumed was the collective biological presence of approximately four thousand people living in an enclosed space. She could not smell the anomaly. She could not smell the fossil. She did not know what she had expected.

The docking bay was busy with the end-of-shift changeover from the outer refinement labs. Workers in processing coveralls moved past her carrying equipment cases, pulling hand-trucks loaded with fossil transport containers, talking — she knew they were talking, could see the movement of mouths, the orientation of bodies toward each other — in the particular pattern of people who did this every day and had run out of things to say about it. Nobody looked at her. She was forty-seven dockings familiar.

Thresh touched her shoulder and pointed toward the Archive intake corridor.

She followed him into the corridor. Behind them, in the pressurized cargo transfer system, the capture pods from the *Meridian* were already moving.

## Archive Intake

The Pan-Systemic Archive's processing facility on Coda occupied most of the inner ring's lower level — a series of climate-controlled labs built around the central fossil vault, where the colony's entire accumulated catalogue of acoustic history was stored in a refrigerated crystalline array that Sable had been shown once, on orientation, and had not thought about much since. It was very large and very cold and contained the compressed acoustic signatures of events spanning approximately four billion years of local galactic history.

The intake desk was staffed by a technician named Prael — a compact, methodical person with short grey hair who had been at the intake desk for as long as Sable had been diving, possibly longer, and who had the air of someone who had seen enough unusual specimens to be genuinely interested in unusual specimens rather than unsettled by them. The desk itself bore the marks of long tenure: a small geode bookend holding a row of physical reference manuals upright, a framed image of a rocky coastline Sable did not recognize propped against the monitor stand, and a mug with a chip in the rim that Prael clearly preferred to its replacement.

Prael was looking at the classification flag on their screen when Sable and Thresh arrived. She could tell from the angle of their body — leaned slightly forward, chin supported on one hand — that this was the expression of someone looking at something that didn't fit the existing organizational schema and finding this more interesting than alarming.

They looked up. *The system has been trying to classify the anomaly since your dive profile uploaded at 11:47,* Prael signed, their colony-standard language slow and broad but readable. *It keeps returning the flag. I ran the scan three times. Crystal density is not registering.*

She signed: *What does that mean?*

*Density measurement requires the system to calculate the compression ratio — how much acoustic data is stored per unit of crystal matrix. For every fossil we've ever processed, this is a finite number. High for stellar events, lower for local impact signatures. The anomaly's compression ratio comes back as —* Prael paused. Their hands reorganized into the configuration for a technical qualifier. *The number is not finite.*

Sable looked at the intake display. The classification field still blank. The flag still reading MANUAL REVIEW REQUIRED.

*Not finite,* she signed.

*The system interprets it as a calculation error. I'm treating it as a classification anomaly pending specialist review from the central Archive.*

*When does the specialist arrive?*

*Four to six standard days.*

She looked at the capture pod, now resting in its intake cradle behind the desk, transferred to the Archive's internal handling system. The green status light had followed it from the *Meridian*. The crystal matrix stable. The fossil contained.

*Has anything unusual happened in the facility since the pod transferred?*

Prael tilted their head. The expression was not quite confusion — more the look of someone who had been asked a question that required more careful consideration than it superficially seemed.

*No,* they signed, after a moment. *Why?*

She held their gaze for a second. Then: *Document everything. Any deviation from standard readings in the vault's environmental monitoring. Anything the system flags, however small.*

Prael nodded, slowly, their expression shifting into something more careful.

She signed *thank you* and turned away from the desk.

Thresh fell into step beside her as she moved toward the Archive's main atrium. She was halfway down the intake corridor when she became aware that someone was standing at the far end.

He was standing still in the way of someone who had chosen to stand still rather than arrived at it — a deliberate stillness, an administrative posture, the stance of a person who managed things. He was wearing Archive administrative grey, the cuffs of which had the particular softness of fabric washed many more times than administrative clothing typically survived — the uniform of someone who had been in this role long enough to wear through its institutional crispness. He was approximately fifty-five years old. He had the kind of face that had arrived at its current configuration through a long series of careful decisions, and the eyes in it were watching her with an attention that was precise and held and did not shift to Thresh.

She knew, before Thresh touched her arm and spelled the name against her skin with one finger — M-A-R-E-T — that this was the administrator.

## Maret

His name in the colony's administrative register was Tomás Maret, Senior Curator, Pan-Systemic Archive — Coda Station. She had seen his name on paperwork. Archive administrators did not, as a rule, come to intake corridors to meet dive crews off the dock. They received summary reports. They approved valuations. They occasionally appeared at premium fossil auctions to represent the Archive's institutional interests. They did not stand at the end of corridors at shift change, waiting.

He did not try to sign. He spoke to Thresh, and Thresh's hands translated in the slight lag of live interpretation.

*He says welcome back from the dive. The classification anomaly flagged in the intake documentation came to his attention and he wanted to introduce himself directly.*

She signed to Thresh: *Tell him the intake technician has submitted a specialist request and the fossil is secured in the vault. Standard protocol.*

Thresh translated. Maret listened. His expression did not change. He spoke again.

*He says standard protocol is appropriate. He says he'd like to know one thing — whether the anomaly's signal presented as structured or as ambient during capture.*

She looked at Maret.

The question was wrong. Not factually — structured versus ambient was a real classification axis in acoustic fossil taxonomy, a distinction between wavefronts generated by discrete events and those produced by continuous atmospheric or environmental processes. It was a legitimate question. But it was not a question a Senior Curator asked a dive crew off the dock the same day as the capture. It was a question a specialist asked after reviewing signal analysis data, which did not exist yet because the classification system had been unable to process the fossil. To ask it now meant Maret already understood enough about the anomaly to know that structured-versus-ambient was the relevant axis. To ask it now meant he had a prior model.

She held his gaze for a long moment.

Then she signed to Thresh: *Structured. Highly structured. Tell him the precise characterization is in my post-dive notation and will be available in the full report.*

Thresh translated. Maret received this. His expression remained exactly what it had been — and it was in that exact consistency, in the absence of any micro-response to the word *structured,* that she read the confirmation. He had already known. He had asked to hear her say it, to have her confirm his model against her firsthand account. He was checking. Whatever he knew, or believed he knew, he was now one data point more confident in it.

He spoke again. Thresh translated: *He thanks us for the prompt intake. We're stood down from active dive rotation pending the specialist review. Full pay continuation. He says we deserve the rest after a heavy harvest.*

She kept her expression neutral. *Tell him we appreciate it.*

She walked past him. She did not look back.

In the atrium, the colony's ambient vibrations surrounded her — the familiar composite signature of four thousand people and their systems and their air processors and their elevators and their fossil refinement equipment, all of it communicated through the structure of the station into the soles of her boots and the palms of her hands where they touched the handrail on the atrium stairs. Known. Mapped. Forty-seven dockings deep in her body's memory.

She climbed the stairs to the residential level and did not think about the one thing she was thinking about, which was the expression Maret had not had when she said the word *structured.*

## The Photograph

Her quarters on Coda were small and precisely arranged in the way of spaces occupied by someone who spent most of their working time in a five-meter dive cabin and had never seen the value of spreading out. A sleeping platform, a desk, a compact sanitation unit, a single shelf. The shelf held four things: a technical manual for the Mark IV dermal transducer array that she had read cover to cover twice and kept for reference, a water glass that lived in the same position for reasons she had never examined, a rectangular piece of matte black composite approximately thirty centimeters by twenty that she had bought at a market on a transit station three years ago because it had a texture she liked — a fine grain, consistent, neither warm nor cold — and a photograph.

The photograph was printed on physical paper, which was unusual enough in the current era to count as an affectation, though she had not thought of it that way when she acquired it. It showed two people standing on the observation deck of a transit hub, backlit by the hub's illuminated arrival board, faces slightly overexposed by the camera's compensation for the bright background. The person on the left was her. The person on the right had been her mother, who had died four years ago in a pressure system failure aboard a refinement barge she should not have been on because she was three months from retirement.

The photograph was the only image she had of her mother's face that she trusted completely. Her memory of her mother's face was reliable — she had studied it sufficiently — but visual memory degraded in ways she could not fully track. The photograph did not degrade.

She had never heard her mother's voice. She had never heard anyone's voice. But she had a phantom memory — had always had it, as long as she could remember — of something she could not name, something that lived in the part of her nervous system that the transducers occupied, a warmth and a texture she associated with her mother in the way she associated everything with sensation. She knew it was neurologically impossible. She had looked it up, twice, in different medical databases, at different points in her adult life. The auditory cortex of a person born profoundly deaf did not process auditory information. The phantom was a misfiled signal, a processing artifact, something her brain had categorized incorrectly in early development. This was the clinical explanation and she had accepted it.

She looked at the photograph now — set her bag on the desk, sat on the edge of the sleeping platform, looked at the shelf with the four things in their four positions — and she felt the transducers running warm, and she thought about the shape that had pressed through her nervous system with the character of *intention,* and she thought: *misfiled signal.*

She looked at the photograph for a long time.

Then she went to the sanitation unit and ran cold water over her hands for approximately ninety seconds — a habit she had developed for recalibrating fingertip node sensitivity after a dive, the thermal contrast helping the nodes reset their baseline — and came back and sat on the sleeping platform and closed her eyes and ran a manual diagnostic through the transducer array. Seven spinal nodes, ten fingertip nodes. The spinal nodes were still warm. The fingertip nodes were at normal operating temperature following the recalibration, except for the two on her right hand flagging reduced sensitivity, already noted and filed.

She lay down. She did not expect to sleep quickly but she was tired in the specific way of someone whose nervous system had processed more information in a single thirty-seven-minute trawl than it usually processed in a week, and she was asleep before she had time to catalogue the sensations of the sleeping platform's familiar pressure distribution.

She did not know how long she slept.

She came awake because something changed.

Not a vibration. The sleeping platform's pressure distribution was the same. The ambient vibrations of the colony's systems were the same. The air against her skin was the same temperature it had been when she lay down.

What had changed was the shelf.

She was looking at the shelf before she had fully decided to open her eyes, some pre-cognitive process having already flagged the thing and directed her attention to it. The four objects were in their four positions. The technical manual. The water glass. The composite rectangle. The photograph.

The photograph was not the same.

She sat up. She moved to the shelf. She picked up the photograph and held it at the angle that gave her the best view under the room's low ambient lighting, and she looked at it carefully — the way she had looked at the condensate droplet on the *Meridian's* port wall, the way she had looked at the rust that bloomed and was not there.

The photograph showed two people standing on an observation deck. Backlit by an arrival board. Faces slightly overexposed.

The person on the left was her.

The person on the right was wrong.

Her hands tightened on the photograph's edges. She felt the pressure through her fingertip transducers — her own grip, registering as data, and beneath the data something that was not data at all. A contraction in her chest, brief and involuntary. She breathed once, deliberately, and loosened her fingers.

Not absent — the figure was still there, same height, same posture, same slight angle of the head she had memorized. The overexposure was the same. But the face — the face she had studied until she trusted it more than her own memory of it — was not her mother's face. It was a face she had never seen. A woman's face, angular and precise, with an expression she could not name because she had never learned to name the expression on the face of someone who was, at the moment the photograph was taken, terrified.

She stood very still with the photograph in her hands.

The figure in the photograph was the same height as her mother. The posture was the same. The clothing was the same clothing her mother had been wearing that day, which Sable remembered because she had been standing next to her. Everything about the figure except the face was her mother.

The face was someone she had never met.

She set the photograph back on the shelf with a precision she was not fully in control of — placing it in the exact position it had occupied, as if precision could hold something in place that was no longer quite where it had been.

She sat down on the sleeping platform and folded her hands in her lap and ran the inventory.

Three symptoms. The condensate inversion: a drop of water falling upward in a column of normal gravity, at eighty-eight atmospheres, immediately post-capture. Aboard the *Meridian,* in the direct physical environment of the capture pod. The rust bloom: approximately eight centimeters of accelerated oxidation on a hull seam, appearing and resolving in approximately two seconds, at eighty-eight atmospheres, immediately post-capture. Aboard the *Meridian,* in the direct physical environment of the capture pod.

The photograph: a substitution of biographical identity in a fixed physical record. Not aboard the *Meridian.* In her quarters. In a room where the capture pod was not, where the fossil was not, where the only thing connecting this room to the lower atmosphere of Vespers was the warmth running through her transducers and whatever she had carried back in her nervous system from the moment the signal returned.

She was in the causal chain. She was the vector.

She sat with this for a while. Then she picked up the photograph again and looked at the face she had never seen, and she thought about the question Maret had asked — *structured or ambient* — and she thought about the precision with which he had not reacted when she said *structured,* and she thought about the phantom memory she had always had and never been able to explain, the warmth and the texture, the misfiled signal that her auditory cortex was not supposed to be able to produce.

The face in the photograph was terrified. Not of anything visible in the frame — the observation deck was ordinary, the other travelers in the soft background blur were ordinary, the arrival board was ordinary. The terror was not situational. It was the terror of someone who understood their situation completely and found that understanding worse than not knowing.

Sable looked at the face for a long time.

She did not know whose face it was. She knew, with a precision she could not yet justify, that this was not a random substitution. She knew, with the same certainty she brought to reading a supernova's shockwave in the spiraling pressure against her finger pads, that whoever this face belonged to had been trying, at the moment it appeared in the frame of her photograph, to tell her something.

She put the photograph face-down on the shelf.

She lay back down on the sleeping platform.

The transducers ran warm. The colony's systems hummed their known frequencies through the floor and the walls and the platform's frame. Outside the colony's hull, the gas giant Vespers moved through its geological time, its crystalline storms trapping the acoustic signatures of the universe's history the way amber traps the remnants of things that have no other way to last.

In the Archive vault on the inner ring's lower level, forty meters of corridors and pressure doors away, a crystal matrix held at controlled temperature maintained the stability of something that was not a sound. The classification system had been trying to process it for six hours. The number it kept returning was not finite.

The warmth in her spine did not diminish.

She did not sleep again.

<!-- PAGE BREAK -->

# Chapter 3 — Transmission

# No Sleep

She hadn't slept again. The colony's systems cycled through their overnight protocols — she could feel the shift in the atmospheric processor frequency as they moved to reduced load, a slightly slower pulse than the daytime operation, which she had catalogued somewhere around her fifth docking without meaning to — and she lay on the sleeping platform and ran the inventory again. Not because it produced new conclusions. Because running the inventory was what she did when she had a problem she couldn't yet solve: she enumerated the known data until the shape of the unknown became cleaner by contrast.

Three symptoms before docking. One since. The rate was not accelerating yet, but she had no baseline for what acceleration would look like because she had no prior model for any of this. She had Maret's prior model, or the implication of one, which was not the same thing as having it.

At 03:11 she stopped running the inventory and sat up.

The photograph was still face-down on the shelf. She did not turn it over.

She dressed. She put on her boots and laced them by feel in the dark, which she always did because the alternative was turning on the overhead light and she had never been able to fall back asleep after overhead light. She picked up her jacket from the desk and was at the door before she had consciously decided to go anywhere, which meant some lower-level part of her processing had already determined the next step and was simply waiting for the rest of her to arrive at it.

She needed to tell Thresh.

## Thresh

The residential corridors on Coda's inner ring ran in a long arc, the curvature of the structure giving each hallway a slight upward slope in one direction and downward in the other that she had learned to use as navigation by feel. Thresh's quarters were seventeen doors down from hers in the downhill direction — she had never counted deliberately, but she had walked it enough times that her feet knew the distance before her conscious mind did. The corridor was empty. The overhead lights had shifted to night mode, a dimmer amber than the daytime setting, and the colony's vibration signature was different at 03:00 — quieter, slower, the particular stillness of a place that was not quite asleep but had stopped pretending to be fully awake.

She stopped at his door and pressed her palm flat against it — the standard attention signal they had developed for situations where knocking was inadequate and lights-on was too much. The door transmitted the vibration in three short pulses, which he would feel through the floor of his quarters.

Forty seconds. Then the door opened.

Thresh looked at her with the specific expression of someone who had not been fully asleep. He was still dressed — or dressed again, she couldn't tell — and his hair was doing something irregular on the left side, and his eyes moved from her face to the empty corridor behind her and back, taking inventory the way she had been taking inventory for the past four hours.

His hands: *How long have you been awake?*

*Since I slept,* she signed.

A pause. Then: *Come in.*

His quarters were slightly larger than hers, occupied by the particular entropy of a person who was meticulous about his tools and unconcerned about everything else. Equipment cases were stacked in a precise column along one wall. The desk held three tablets and a partially disassembled depth gauge and two coffee cups that were not clean. The sleeping platform had a jacket on it, which confirmed the dressed-again hypothesis. He moved the jacket and gestured toward the platform and she sat on the desk chair instead, because the desk chair put her back to the wall and she wanted to be facing the room.

He sat on the platform and looked at her and waited. Thresh was good at waiting. It was, she had decided years ago, the most important quality in someone who worked with her — not patience in the vague motivational sense, but a genuine absence of the need to fill silence, to prompt, to rush the conclusion. He understood that she was always thinking, that the pause before she signed was not hesitation but composition, and he had never once in three seasons tried to complete her sentences.

She signed: *I am the vector.*

She let him read that before she continued. His expression did not change much, but she saw the slight shift around his eyes that meant he was processing something that had confirmed rather than surprised him — that he had already been in the vicinity of this conclusion and she had just handed him the last piece.

She laid it out in sequence. The four symptoms. Their locations. The relationship between the fossil's position and the first three, the break in that relationship for the fourth. The transducers running warm. The logical implication.

*The Resonance is not radiating from the fossil,* she signed. *Or not only from the fossil. Something from the capture transferred through the hardware into my nervous system and I brought it to the colony in my spine.*

Thresh's hands: *The warm transducers.*

*Yes.*

*Since capture.*

*Yes.*

He looked at his hands for a moment, which was what he did when he was organizing a response that had more components than usual.

*Then it's not contained in the vault,* he signed. *Whatever the Archive thinks it's holding, it's not holding all of it.*

*No,* she signed. *It is not.*

He stood up. Not agitated — Thresh did not get agitated; he simply moved from sitting to standing when sitting was no longer the correct posture for the situation. He went to the desk and picked up one of the tablets and turned it toward her. She read the screen.

It was a maintenance notification from the Archive's environmental monitoring system, logged at 02:47 local time. She read the notation: *VAULT TEMPERATURE ANOMALY — SECTOR 4C — LOCALIZED COLD SPOT, -12°C BELOW AMBIENT, DURATION 3 MINUTES 41 SECONDS — SELF-RESOLVED — FLAGGED FOR TECHNICIAN REVIEW.*

Vault temperature anomalies were not common, but they were not unprecedented — the refrigeration system ran in zones and occasional fluctuations occurred at zone boundaries. Twelve degrees below ambient was outside the normal fluctuation range. Three minutes forty-one seconds was long enough to be significant. Self-resolved meant no technician had intervened.

*When did you get this?* she signed.

*It routes to all active crew with recent Archive intake. Standard notification.*

She handed him the tablet back. *Prael was supposed to be monitoring.*

*They probably were. Self-resolved means the system logged it and moved on.*

She thought about Prael's expression when she had said *document everything* — the slight shift from interested to careful. Prael was documenting. Whether Prael had flagged it upward was another question.

*We need to go to the outer ring,* she signed.

Thresh looked at the tablet. Then at her. His hands: *Why?*

She didn't have a clean answer. What she had was a pattern-recognition operating below the threshold of enumerable data — that the vault anomaly and the photograph and the warm transducers were pointing at something that was not staying in one place.

*Because the fossil didn't stay in one place,* she signed. *And neither did I.*

## Refinement Labs

The outer ring at 03:30 was not empty. The acoustic fossil refinement labs ran a continuous processing cycle — the crystalline matrix of newly harvested fossils required temperature management that didn't respect shift changes, and the technicians who worked the overnight rotation were a specific type of person: methodical, self-contained, comfortable with the particular isolation of being responsible for things that had taken billions of years to form and could be destroyed in seconds by a temperature excursion or a handling error.

The refinement labs occupied a crescent of the outer ring's lower level, separated from the main corridor by pressure doors that were always slightly ajar during active processing to allow the ventilation system to manage the temperature differential between the refrigerated lab interior and the corridor. She and Thresh walked the corridor in the amber of the night lights, and she was running the ambient vibration signature against her memory of previous night-shift walks on this corridor when she felt something change.

Not a dramatic change. A frequency — not from the floor, not from the walls, but from behind the pressure door of lab seven, through the door itself as she passed it because the door was slightly ajar and she was close enough to the gap.

She stopped.

The vibration from lab seven was wrong. Not wrong in the way of a mechanical problem — she knew the signature of equipment malfunction, the arrhythmic pulse of a failing component, and this was not that. It was wrong in the way the photograph had been wrong: internally consistent, precisely the wrong thing, with the quality of a substitution rather than a failure. Something in lab seven was doing exactly what it was supposed to do. It was doing it in a direction she did not have a technical term for.

She put her hand on Thresh's arm. He stopped. She pointed at the door.

He stepped to the gap and pressed two fingers against it, reading the vibration in the way she had taught him years ago — not with her precision, but with enough sensitivity to confirm that something was happening.

His hands: *The processing array?*

She nodded.

*Running backwards?*

She looked at him. He had felt it without her framing it. She held up one hand — *wait* — and pushed the pressure door open.

The lab interior was lit by work lights positioned over the processing array — a series of glass-enclosed chambers along the far wall, each containing a fossil in its crystal matrix, each connected to the array's temperature management and acoustic analysis systems by a network of sensor filaments. The overnight technician was standing approximately three meters from the array with her back to the door. She turned at the change in the room's vibration when the door opened, and her face was doing something.

She was not injured. She was not in distress in any physical sense. But her expression had the quality of someone who had been presented with something outside their operational framework and had not yet found a framework to replace it with.

Sable crossed to the monitoring desk. The overnight log was on the screen. She read it.

The processing array had been running a standard acoustic analysis on three fossils — all routine harvest material brought in two weeks ago, all previously classified, awaiting completion of the Archive's provenance documentation. The analysis system read the frequency content of each fossil's crystalline matrix and translated it into standard acoustic notation: a visual spectrogram that human analysts could review for classification confirmation.

All three fossils were displayed on the monitoring screen. Two of them looked exactly as they should — the familiar spectrographic patterns she had seen on a hundred post-harvest documentation sets. Stellar formation on the left, asteroid impact on the right.

The middle fossil was displaying its spectrogram in reverse.

Not the spectrogram reversed — the spectrogram was the same shape it had been two weeks ago, the same pattern. But the time axis of the display was running backward. The interval markers were moving from right to left rather than left to right, the analysis time counter decrementing rather than incrementing.

The fossil's acoustic content was running backward. Not playing backward — the crystal matrix was stable, the physical object unchanged. The analysis system's read of the content was reversed. The system was reading a fossil that contained a forward-direction acoustic event as if the event were running in reverse, as if time within the crystal were flowing in the opposite direction from time outside it.

She looked at this for a long moment. Then she looked at the overnight technician.

The technician's name badge read SEVI. Her expression said she had been looking at this for approximately as long as it would take to convince yourself twice that you were not misreading the display, and that after the second confirmation she had not known what to do and had stood very still instead.

Sable signed to Thresh: *Tell her to log it exactly as she's reading it. Don't correct for it. Don't restart the analysis. Log the output as received.*

Thresh spoke. Sevi looked at Sable, and her expression shifted from the helplessness of a person outside their framework to the relief of a person given a procedurally correct thing to do. She went to the monitoring desk and began logging.

Sable looked at the middle fossil through the glass of its chamber. The crystal matrix was intact. The status indicator was green. The fossil was, by every physical measure, unchanged.

The time axis on its spectrogram continued to run backward.

She turned to Thresh. *How many labs are running overnight analysis?*

His hands: *Probably four or five. Labs three, seven, nine, and twelve usually run continuous.*

She looked at the pressure door. Three other labs.

*We should check them,* she signed.

---

Labs three and twelve were normal. Lab nine had a single fossil displaying a spectrogram in a language — she had no better word for it — that the analysis system did not recognize. Not reversed, not corrupted, not a display error. The system was reading the fossil's acoustic content correctly, translating it accurately into visual notation, and the notation was a pattern of frequency and amplitude that did not correspond to any event type in the Archive's classification database. Not a known event type rendered unusually. Something else. Something the database did not contain a category for.

She logged it and left it running and told the technician in lab nine the same thing she had told Sevi: log exactly what you see, do not correct for it, do not restart.

Walking back through the corridor toward the inner ring, she was running the implications in sequence. The Resonance had reached the refinement labs. The fossil vault was in the inner ring, forty meters of corridors away from the outer ring. Between the vault and the labs, the only physical link was the cargo transfer system, which moved raw harvest material, not fossil vault contents.

The Resonance was not moving through the physical infrastructure.

She was the only person who had been in the lower atmosphere of Vespers in the past twenty-four hours. She was the only person who had been in direct contact with the anomaly. She was the only person with warm transducers.

*But I wasn't in the refinement labs tonight,* she signed to Thresh, as they reached the pressure door between the outer and inner ring. *I was in my quarters. And then I was with you.*

He stopped walking. She could see him working through the same implication.

*The Resonance doesn't require your proximity anymore,* he signed. *You were the initial vector. Now it's spreading on its own.*

She nodded. This was the conclusion she had been building toward for the past hour, and it was worse than the vector hypothesis, which had implied containment. Self-propagating meant it no longer required her. It had already made the jump.

Stage one: inanimate matter. Stage two: recorded media. The fossils in the refinement labs were recorded media — ancient acoustic recordings stored in crystal. The reversal of time axes. The unclassifiable frequency pattern. Precisely what Stage 2 looked like.

Stage two had already arrived.

She pushed through the pressure door into the inner ring. She needed to find Thresh's sister.

## Senne

Senne worked the analysis division of the outer ring's refinement facility, which meant she was a day-shift employee who would not begin her rotation for another four hours. She lived three floors above the residential level in a section of the inner ring built during the colony's second expansion phase — the ceilings were fractionally lower there, a consequence of the structural modifications required to add the upper residential levels to the existing framework, and Sable had learned to notice the change in the vibration profile when she crossed into this section because the lower ceilings altered the resonance characteristics of the corridor in a way her transducers registered as a slight increase in ambient texture density.

The vibration profile was the same as always. This was either reassuring or insufficient.

She pressed her palm to Senne's door in three short pulses. Thresh stood behind her and slightly to the left, which was where he always stood when he was uncertain how a situation was going to resolve — not defensively, not blocking, but positioned to move in any necessary direction.

The door opened in approximately twenty seconds, which meant Senne had also not been fully asleep.

She was slightly taller than Thresh and three years younger, with the same cast to her jaw and the same quality of stillness, but where Thresh's stillness was operational — the stillness of a person who moved precisely when movement served a purpose — Senne's had an extra layer of deliberateness to it, the stillness of someone who had spent years learning to be in rooms without dominating them. She had chosen analysis work, Thresh had told Sable once, because she found the decay signatures beautiful — the way time recorded itself in the slow degradation of high-frequency harmonics. *It's like reading tree rings,* she had said, *except the trees were stars.* She communicated in a mix of the colony standard and the modified cabin language she had learned from Thresh, which meant Sable's comprehension of her was higher than it was for most people on the station.

She looked at Sable, then at Thresh, then at the corridor behind them, then back at Sable.

Her hands: *Is this about the fossil?*

Sable looked at her. *What do you know about the fossil?*

*I know the classification system can't read it. Prael told me.* A pause. *I was at the intake desk when the pod transferred. I helped with the physical relay from the cargo system.*

Sable felt something in her chest that was not a transducer reading. She kept her expression level. *When you say helped with the transfer — how close were you to the pod?*

Senne's hands: *I handled the pod. Both hands. Standard cargo relay. Why?*

She looked at Thresh. He was looking at his sister. She could see the specific rearrangement happening in his face — the moment of understanding arriving and being received and being held very carefully, the way you held something you knew was fragile.

He looked at Sable. His face was asking a question he was not ready to sign yet.

She looked back at Senne. *Has anything seemed wrong today? Anything in your quarters, your equipment, your routine that didn't match what you expected?*

Senne considered this with the seriousness of a person who understood that the question was not casual.

*There was a recording,* she signed, slowly. *A reference recording I use for calibration. I've had it for two years. A stellar formation event, class three, standard reference material. I ran it this morning for a calibration check and the output was the same recording, same event, same frequency profile — but the decay signature was wrong. Acoustic decay is how we estimate fossil age. The older the event, the more the high-frequency components have degraded. This recording's decay signature read as something much older than it should have been. The same recording I've run two hundred times, but the system read it as if the event happened much further back.*

Sable kept her hands still. *How much further back?*

*The system wouldn't give me a number. It flagged the decay signature as out-of-range for the known classification.* Senne paused. *I thought it was a calibration error. I was going to run a hardware diagnostic in the morning.*

*Don't correct for it,* Sable signed. *Log exactly what the system reports. Don't restart, don't recalibrate.*

Senne looked at her for a moment with the expression of someone revising their understanding of the scope of a problem they had thought was small.

*What is it?* she signed.

Sable thought about the shape that had pressed through her nervous system with the character of intention. She thought about the face in the photograph, the terror of someone who understood completely.

*I don't know yet,* she signed. *I'm working on it.*

They stayed for another hour. Senne made tea and distributed the containers — she said the act of handling them helped her think — and Sable held hers without drinking it and felt the warmth against her palms, which were already running their own warmth from the transducers, and she thought about Senne handling the capture pod with both hands and she thought about the decay signature reading wrong and she thought about Stage 3 and tried to calculate how much time she had before the colony's short-term biological memory started showing the same distortions the fossils were already showing in the labs.

She did not arrive at a reassuring number.

She and Thresh left at 05:20. The colony's vibration signature was beginning to shift toward its day-shift profile — the freight elevator making its first full run of the morning, the atmospheric processors cycling up, the outer ring labs beginning their transition. She walked with Thresh as far as the junction between their corridors.

His hands, at the junction: *I'll check on her every shift.*

She signed: *Yes.*

*She doesn't know what this means.*

*No.*

*But you do.* His hands stayed up — not finished. *When does documenting become doing something?*

She held his gaze. It was the first time in three seasons he had questioned her method rather than her conclusion. She thought about what she knew, which was: a five-stage escalation, a classification system outputting non-finite numbers, a vault temperature anomaly, a photograph with the wrong face, refinement fossils with reversed time axes, a calibration recording that had aged ten billion years overnight, and a man in administrative grey who had asked the right question in the wrong moment because he already knew the answer. She thought about what she did not know, which was: how to make any of that knowledge into something that would bring Senne's lost time back.

*I know enough,* she signed. *Not enough yet.*

He held her gaze for a moment. The expression she had seen on his face for three years — the one that meant he trusted her and wished he didn't have to — was still there, but it had acquired an edge, a thinness, the look of something that was being spent faster than it was being earned.

Then he went down his corridor and she went down hers and the colony shifted into its morning frequency and the transducers in her spine ran warm all the way to her door.

## The Access Log

She was at her desk, writing up the night's observations in the systematic notation she used for post-dive logs, when the tablet lit with an incoming access notification.

She had set up the notification after Maret stood her down from the rotation — a simple system flag on her post-dive report file that would alert her to any external access. Not standard procedure. Something she had done in the same quiet, unhurried way she had told Prael to log everything: not because she had proof of a specific threat, but because the pattern of available data made preparation rational.

The notification read: *FILE ACCESSED — POST-DIVE REPORT REF. 7744-K — 03:47 LOCAL — CREDENTIAL: ARCHIVE ADMINISTRATIVE ACCESS, LEVEL 5 — USER ID: [REDACTED].*

She looked at this for a moment. The access had occurred at 03:47 — eighteen minutes after she had left her quarters to go to Thresh's. The credential was Archive administrative, Level 5, which was senior curatorial access. There were exactly two Level 5 credentials active on Coda Station.

One of them was Maret.

The user ID was redacted, which was not a standard feature of the access log system. She had reviewed her access logs after previous dives and the user ID had always been visible. The redaction was not a system default. It was a manual modification, applied to this specific access event, after the fact.

Maret had read her post-dive report at 03:47 while she was in Thresh's quarters, and had then gone back into the access log and redacted his own credential.

He had not, however, removed the access event itself. He had not deleted the notification trigger she had set. He had not removed the timestamp, or the credential level, or the file reference.

She looked at the notification for a long time.

There were two explanations. The first was that he had made an error — that the redaction had been partial, that he had removed his name but not noticed the remaining data. She did not find this convincing. A man who asked the right question with the wrong timing and held his expression perfectly still when she said *structured* was not a man who made careless errors.

The second explanation was that he had left it deliberately. That the redaction was not an attempt at concealment but a message: *I know you have monitoring active. I know you will see this. I am telling you that I am reading your reports and I am telling you that I am not telling you who I am, and I am leaving you the negative space of that choice to interpret.*

He was talking to her. Through the access log. In the language of what he had not done, which she was, unfortunately, fluent in.

She closed the notification. She opened a new file. She began logging the night's observations in full — the refinement labs, the reversed time axis in lab seven, the unclassifiable frequency pattern in lab nine, Senne's miscalibrated decay signature, the access notification with its redacted credential.

She logged it all with the same precision she brought to post-dive notation. When she was done she looked at what she had written and she thought: *this is what Stage 2 looks like.* Recorded media. The fossils running backward in their chambers, reading their age wrong, playing in a direction the universe did not currently support.

She thought about how long she had before Stage 3.

Then she closed the file and set it to encrypted local storage only — no Archive network access — and went to make coffee, and stood in the small kitchen area feeling the morning vibration of the colony against the soles of her feet, and thought about Maret reading her report at 03:47 and the face in the photograph and the word *structured* and the silence that had followed it.

She thought: *he knows what it is.*

She thought: *I need to know what he knows.*

She thought: *I need to know it before Stage 3.*

The coffee was ready. She drank it standing up, looking at the shelf with the four objects in their four positions. The photograph still face-down. She did not turn it over.

She was almost certain that if she did, the face would still be there.

She was less certain than she would have liked that it would still be terrified, and not something else.

<!-- PAGE BREAK -->

# Chapter 4 — The Gap

## The Missing Station

The Archive's public classification index was not a document most people on Coda had reason to access. It was a reference system — a master catalogue of every acoustic fossil ever processed by any Pan-Systemic Archive station across all operational colonies, organized by station designation, processing year, and fossil classification type. It existed primarily for provenance verification: when a fossil changed hands commercially, when a researcher needed to confirm the origin station and processing date of a specimen, when an auditor ran the annual compliance review.
